\alpha_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}=-\Bigl(\eta_{t}X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}+h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],

where

(i)

{\bm{\eta}}=(\eta_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
solves the Riccati equation:

(2.3)

\dot{\eta}_{t}-\eta_{t}^{2}+Q^{\dagger}Q=0,\quad t\in[0,T]\,;\quad\eta_{T}=R^{\dagger}R;

(ii)

{\bm{h}}^{n+1}=(h_{t}^{n+1})_{0\leq t\leq T}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d}({\mathbb{F}}^{{\bm{W}}})
solves the backward SDE:

(2.4)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}h_{t}^{n+1}=\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}Q^{\dagger}f(\overline{m}^{n}_{t})+\eta_{t}h_{t}^{n+1}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}^{n+1}\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&h_{T}^{n+1}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}R^{\dagger}g\bigl(\overline{m}^{n}_{T}\bigr);\end{split}

(iii)

{\bm{X}}^{n+1,\varpi}=(X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi})_{0\leq t\leq T}
solves the forward SDE:

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}=-\Bigl(\eta_{t}X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}+h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}},\quad t\in[0,T];\quad X_{0}^{n+1,\varpi}=X_{0}.

The statement makes it possible to let

(2.5)

m^{n+1}_{t}:={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}})\bigr],\quad t\in[0,T];\quad m_{0}^{n+1}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}),

and then, consistently with
(
2.2
),

(2.6)

\overline{m}^{n+1}_{t}:=\tfrac{\varpi(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\varpi^{-k}m_{t}^{k}=\tfrac{\varpi^{-n}(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}m^{n+1}_{t}+\Bigl(1-\tfrac{\varpi^{-n}(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}\Bigr)\overline{m}^{n}_{t}.

Remark 2.2
.

Notice that because the density
{\mathcal{E}}(\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon)
is
\sigma({\bm{W}})
-measurable, we also have

m^{n+1}_{t}={\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigl[X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}})\bigr],\quad t\in[0,T],

which is a direct consequence of Bayes’ rule, see
[
53
, Exercise 23.14]
.

Remark 2.3
.

Although each

\overline{\bm{m}}^{n}
depends in an obvious manner on

\varpi
, this is our choice not to add
\varpi
as a label in
the notation. The reason is that the limit is independent of
\varpi
,
as clarified in §
2.1.2

below.
Similarly,
we do not put
\varpi
in
the notation
{\bm{h}}^{n}
: as shown in Theorem

2.4
, the limit is independent of
\varpi
up to a rescaling by
\varpi
.
This is in contrast with
the quantities
{\bm{X}}^{n,\varpi}

and
{\bm{\alpha}}^{n,\varpi}
:
the limits depend on
\varpi
in a non-trivial manner,
which we also make clear in
the next paragraph.

On another matter, it must be noticed
that Lemma

2.1

remains valid when

\varpi=1
. As explained in
Introduction, see
(
1.29
), the updating rate
in
(
2.6
)
must then be understood
as
1/(n+1)
and, accordingly,
the
formula
(
2.2
)
coincides with
the harmonic updating rule
(
1.22
).
This remark is important in order to compare the two harmonic and
geometric schemes.

2.1.2.
Main statement

As noticed in the introduction,
it is especially convenient to reformulate the dynamics of

{\bm{X}}^{n+1,\varpi}
under the historical probability. Quite clearly, we can write:

(2.7)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}=-\Bigl(\eta_{t}X_{t}^{n+1,\varpi}+h_{t}^{n+1}-{\varpi}h_{t}^{n}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T];\quad X_{0}^{n+1,\varpi}=X_{0},

and then

(2.8)

\mathrm{d}m_{t}^{n+1}=-\Bigl(\eta_{t}m_{t}^{n+1}+h_{t}^{n+1}-{\varpi}h_{t}^{n}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T];\quad m_{0}^{n+1}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}).

By dividing (
2.8
) by
\varpi^{n+1}
, then summing over the index
n
and eventually multiplying by
\varpi(1-\varpi^{-1})/(1-\varpi^{-(n+1)})
, we get:

(2.9)

\mathrm{d}\overline{m}_{t}^{n+1}=-\Bigl(\eta_{t}\overline{m}_{t}^{n+1}+\tfrac{(1-\varpi^{-1})\varpi^{-n}}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T].

By coupling
with the backward equation
in the statement of Lemma

2.1
(when reformulated under the historical probability),
we obtain the forward-backward system:

(2.10)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\overline{m}_{t}^{n+1}=-\Bigl(\eta_{t}\overline{m}_{t}^{n+1}+\tfrac{(1-\varpi^{-1})\varpi^{-n}}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t},\\
&\mathrm{d}h_{t}^{n+1}=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{t}^{n}\bigr)+\eta_{t}h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+{\varpi}k_{t}^{n+1}h^{n}_{t}\mathrm{d}t+{\varepsilon}k_{t}^{n+1}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&h_{T}^{n+1}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}R^{\dagger}g\bigl(\overline{m}^{n}_{T}\bigr).\end{split}

Our main result in this regard is the following statement:

Theorem 2.4
.

The scheme (
2.10
) converges
to the decoupled version of the FBSDE system:

(2.11)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}m_{t}=-\eta_{t}m_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t},\\
&\mathrm{d}h_{t}^{\varpi}=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}Q^{\dagger}f(m_{t})+\eta_{t}h_{t}^{\varpi}+{\varpi}k_{t}^{\varpi}h_{t}^{\varpi}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}^{\varpi}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&m_{0}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}),\quad h_{T}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}R^{\dagger}g(m_{T}),\end{split}

with an explicit bound on the rate of convergence, namely

(2.12)

\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega}\Bigl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Bigl(|m_{t}-\overline{m}_{t}^{n}|^{2}+|h_{t}^{\varpi}-h_{t}^{n}|^{2}\Bigr)\Bigr]\leq\varpi^{-2n}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr),

for a constant
C
that depends on
d
,
T
and the norms

\|Q^{\dagger}f\|_{1,\infty}
and
\|R^{\dagger}g\|_{1,\infty}
.

Moreover, up to a modification of the constant
C
, the weak error of the scheme for the
Fortet-Mourier distance satisfies:

(2.13)

\begin{split}&\sup_{F}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[F\bigl(\overline{\bm{m}}^{n},{\bm{h}}^{n}\bigr)\Bigr]-{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{\varpi}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[F\bigl({\bm{m}},{\bm{h}}\bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr|\leq\varpi^{-n}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr),\end{split}

the supremum in the left-hand side being taken over all the functions

F
on
{\mathcal{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
that are
bounded by
1
and
1
-Lipschitz continuous.

Importantly (and this is the interest of the result),
the
solution
(m_{t},\varpi h_{t}^{\varpi})_{0\leq t\leq T}
of the system (
2.11
) should be regarded as a solution of the (original) MFG with common noise
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
)–(
1.3
), whenever the latter is formulated in the weak form.
Indeed,
for
{\bm{m}}=(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

and

{\bm{h}}:=\varpi{\bm{h}}^{\varpi}=(\varpi h^{\varpi}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

as in
(
2.11
),
the optimal path
(X_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
associated with the minimization problem

(2.14)

\textrm{\rm inf}_{\bm{\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\Bigl[{\mathcal{R}}^{X_{0}}\Bigl({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{m}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}{\bm{W}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\Bigr)\Bigr]

has exactly
{\bm{m}}=(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
as conditional expectation given the common noise, which
follows from an obvious adaptation of Lemma

2.1
. Namely,

m_{t}={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[X_{t}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}})\bigr],\quad t\in[0,T],

where
(X_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is the optimal trajectory to the latter cost functional.
As before,
this can be rewritten as

m_{t}={\mathbb{E}}^{\bm{h}/\varepsilon}\bigl[X_{t}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}})\bigr],\quad t\in[0,T].

Indeed, under
{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
,
the process
({\bm{m}},{\bm{h}})
satisfies the following forward-backward system, which characterizes the
conditional expectation of the
optimal trajectory to (
2.14
):

(2.15)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}m_{t}=-\eta_{t}m_{t}\mathrm{d}t-h_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},\\
&\mathrm{d}h_{t}=\bigl(-Q^{\dagger}f(m_{t})+\eta_{t}h_{t}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&m_{0}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}),\quad h_{T}=R^{\dagger}g(m_{T}).\end{split}

To derive the above system, it suffices to write it
first
under
{\mathbb{P}}
:

(2.16)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}m_{t}=-\eta_{t}m_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t},\\
&\mathrm{d}h_{t}=\bigl(-Q^{\dagger}f(m_{t})+\eta_{t}h_{t}+k_{t}h_{t}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&m_{0}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}),\quad h_{T}=R^{\dagger}g(m_{T}).\end{split}

By the changes of variable
(with the first one being already
defined in
the paragraph preceding
(
2.14
))

(2.17)

h_{t}^{\varpi}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t},\quad k_{t}^{\varpi}:=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],

we indeed recover
(
2.11
). As we claimed, this identifies the pair

({\bm{m}},{\bm{h}}=\varpi{\bm{h}}^{\varpi})
as an equilibrium of the original MFG
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
)–(
1.3
).

Noticeably, the two systems
(
2.15
)
and
(
2.16
)
provide two distinct representations of the solution
\theta_{\varepsilon}
to the PDE
(
1.8
).
The connection between the two is given by the identities

(2.18)

h_{t}=\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,m_{t}),\ t\in[0,T]\,;\quad k_{t}=\nabla_{x}\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,m_{t}),\ t\in[0,T),

which holds true
under both
{\mathbb{P}}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
.
By a standard application of the maximum principle (for PDEs),

\theta_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in terms of
d
,
T
,
\|Q^{\dagger}f\|_{1,\infty}

and
\|R^{\dagger}g\|_{1,\infty}
, and, by Lemma

2.8

right below,

\nabla_{x}\theta_{\varepsilon}
is also bounded in terms of
d
,
\varepsilon
,
T
,

\|Q^{\dagger}f\|_{1,\infty}

and
\|R^{\dagger}g\|_{1,\infty}
.
The relationships stated in (
2.18
)
show in fact how to construct easily a solution to
(
2.15
)
and
(
2.16
)
by solving first for
(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
in the forward equation and then by expanding

(\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,m_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
The hence constructed solution
(h_{t},k_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
to the backward equation in
(
2.16
) (equivalently (
2.15
)) is bounded. In turn, uniqueness to the backward equation in
(
2.16
) (equivalently (
2.15
)) is easily shown to hold true in the class of bounded processes

(h_{t},k_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
Similarly,
(
2.11
)
has a unique solution, which is then obtained by the change of variable
(
2.17
).

Remark 2.5
.

The reader will observe that, in the equation
(
2.7
) for the optimal trajectory in the fictitious play, the intensity of the
idiosyncratic noise is
\sigma/\varpi
. This is different from
the intensity of the idiosyncratic noise underpinning the controlled trajectories in the MFG (
1.1
)–(
1.2
)–(
1.3
),
which is
\sigma
. In particular,
the sequence of processes

({\bm{X}}^{n,\varpi})_{n\geq 1}
,
defined in
Lemma

2.1
(see also (
2.7
)), cannot be ‘a good approximation’ of
the process

{\bm{X}}
that minimizes
(
2.14
).
Although this looks paradoxal with the result stated in Theorem

2.4
, it must be clear that, implicitly,
the two bounds (
2.12
)
and (
2.13
)
rely on the fact that
the dynamics for the MFG equilibrium
{\bm{m}}
does not depend on

\sigma
.

Remark 2.6
.

Our construction of a fictitious play
with a geometric learning rate, proportional to
\varpi^{-n}

(see (
2.6
)), as opposed
to the harmonic learning rate
1/(n+1)

that is used in the standard version of the fictitious play,
could be easily extended to
other (neither harmonic nor geometric) rates.
There are two key principles that should be followed:
the first one is to
make appear, in the dynamics
(
2.8
),
a difference
between the
component
{\bm{h}}^{n+1}
of the
scheme at iteration
n+1
and the
component
{\bm{h}}^{n}

of the
scheme
at iteration
n
,
and the second one is to define the tilted measure depending on the form
of the finite difference (which is exactly the case in (
2.1
)).
What is remarkable in this approach is that the tilted measure has a bias. Whereas it would be natural
to take the tilted measure as
{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}
, it is here taken as

{\mathbb{P}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}
with
\varpi\not=1
. The reader will easily see that, in order to
recover the ‘non-biased’ setting (which formally corresponds to
\varpi=1
), one needs to replace

\varpi h^{n}_{t}
by
h^{n}_{t}
in
(
2.7
)
and
(
2.8
).
By summing over
n
as in
(
2.9
), we would then
obtain

1/(n+1)
as learning rate in
(
2.6
). In other words, the ‘non-biased’ regime corresponds to
the harmonic fictitious play.

Although
Theorem

2.4
does not cover the case
\varpi=1
, the interested will easily check that the proof that is given below also works
when
\varpi=1
. Then, the bound
in
(
2.12
)
must be replaced by

\exp\bigl(C\varepsilon^{-2}\bigr)/n^{2}
. Similarly,
(
2.13
)
must be replaced by

\exp\bigl(C\varepsilon^{-2}\bigr)/n
.
In fact, the same remark applies to the forthcoming Theorems

2.17

and

2.27
.

Remark 2.7
.

We notice for later purposes that
the backward equation
in (
2.15
)
can be ‘solved’ explicitly.
Indeed, calling
(P_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
the solution of the linear differential equation

\dot{P}_{t}=-P_{t}\eta_{t}
, for
t\in[0,T]
with
P_{0}=I_{d}
as initial condition,
where
I_{d}
is the
d
-dimensional identity matrix, it holds

\mathrm{d}\bigl[P_{t}h_{t}\bigr]=-P_{t}Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{t}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon P_{t}k_{t}\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},

or equivalently,

\begin{split}&P_{t}h_{t}=P_{T}R^{\dagger}g\bigl({m}_{T}\bigr)+\int_{t}^{T}P_{s}Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{s}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s-\varepsilon\int_{t}^{T}P_{s}k_{s}\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

2.1.3.
Discussion about the rate of convergence

Beside any specific application to learning, this is another of our contributions to provide an explicit bound for the rate of convergence of our variant of the fictitious play for mean field games with common noise.
We feel worth to point out that, to the best of our knowledge, there are very few results on the rate of convergence for the fictitious play in the absence of common noise, whether the mean field game be potential or monotone (as we already explained, no result is available without common noise outside the potential or monotone cases, except
[
66
]
which is for a time-continuous version of the fictitious play).
In most of the existing references, the analysis indeed involves an additional compactness argument
which complicates the computation of the rate.
Still, the reader can find in
[
41
]
an explicit rate for the exploitability for a monotone and potential game set in discrete time; the bound is of order
O(1/\sqrt{n})
(hence weaker than ours). In
[
66
]
, a bound is shown, also for the exploitability, but for the time-continuous version of the fictitious play, when the game is monotone; it is of order
O(1/n)
, and is
also weaker than ours in the geometric setting but is hence comparable to ours in the harmonic framework.

In comparison, the thrust of our analysis is to provide a scheme with a geometric decay.
Obviously,
this must be tempered, due to the presence of the multiplicative constant
\exp(C\varepsilon^{-2})
.
When the intensity
\varepsilon
is away from zero, the effective decay is really good, but when
\varepsilon
gets close to
0
(which is the typical regime when we use the common noise as an exploration noise), the exponential factor really matters. Of course the bound for the error may be rewritten as follows:

\exp\bigl(-n\ln(\varpi)+\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr),

which says that

n
should be chosen on a scale larger than
\varepsilon^{-2}
. We think that
this is numerically affordable. In comparison (see Remark
2.6
), if one had to work with the standard fictitious play
(i.e., with a harmonic learning rate), the same analysis would lead to a bound of
order
\exp(C\varepsilon^{-2})/n
, which is obviously much worse. In the first arXiv version
[
34
]
of this work, dedicated to the analysis of the sole harmonic regime, we claimed that
this was possible to remove the exponential factor, but there was a clear mistake in the computations: for convenience reasons, we decided not to indicate the dependence
upon
\varepsilon
in the various tilted measures, as a consequence of which we forgot a factor
1/\varepsilon
in some of the main estimates.

The presence of the exponential factor comes from the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to the end of the subsection:

Lemma 2.8
.

There exists a constant
C_{1}
, only depending on
d
,
T
,
\|Q^{\dagger}f\|_{1,\infty}

and
\|R^{\dagger}g\|_{1,\infty}
, such that

|\nabla_{x}\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\varepsilon^{2}},\quad t\in[0,T),\ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

with
\theta_{\varepsilon}
the solution of the PDE (
1.8
).

The above
L^{\infty}
bound for the gradient is known
to be sharp, but it looks rather poor because the estimate is precisely given in

L^{\infty}
. Also, one may hope for better estimates in different norms. In other words,
\theta_{\varepsilon}
may indeed become
very steep, but maybe only on some localized parts of the space.
This is the point where things become highly subtle
because
the process
\overline{\bm{m}}^{n}
becomes localized itself as the diffusion coefficient

\varepsilon
tends to
0
. So, the challenging question is to decide whether it may stay or not in parts of the space where the gradient is high.
As exemplified in the
analysis performed in
Delarue and Foguen
[
32
]
, this may be a challenging question, even in dimension 1.
Unless we make additional assumptions on the model
(assuming for instance that
\theta_{\varepsilon}
is smooth independently of
\varepsilon
, which is for example the case when

T
is small enough), we must confess that we are not able to provide a more relevant bound for the
gradient of
\theta_{\varepsilon}
(which bound gives in the end a bound for the process
{\bm{k}}
in
(
2.15
), see
(
2.18
)).

It must be also stressed that
(
2.13
)
provides a bound for the so-called
weak
error
of the scheme
and is fully relevant from the practical point of view.
In our context, the
strong
error, as addressed in
(
2.12
), does not provide the same information. Indeed, because
the two densities

{\mathcal{E}}(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}{\bm{h}})
and
{\mathcal{E}}(\tfrac{\varpi}{\varepsilon}{\bm{h}}^{n})

become singular when
\varepsilon
tends to
0
, the passage from the strong to the weak error is not direct.

Remark 2.9
.

The reader may wonder about the scope
of Theorem
2.4
in the higher dimensional framework.
This question will be addressed from a purely numerical prospect
in the forthcoming Section

3
, see in particular Subsection

3.4
.
From a more theoretical point of view, the same question can be
addressed by investigating the dependence of the constant
C

in (
2.13
) upon the dimension
d
.
Whereas we cannot provide a sharp (or at least reasonable) bound in full generality, we show below that, even in simple cases when
the matrices
Q
and
R
reduce to the
d\times d
identity matrix
and the coefficients
f
and
g
are diagonal, i.e.
each coordinate
i
of
f
(respectively
g
) writes

f^{i}(x)=f_{0}(x_{i})

(respectively

g^{i}(x)=g_{0}(x_{i})
) for a function

f_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

(respectively

g_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
),
with
x_{i}
denoting the
i^{\rm th}
coordinate of
x
, then the
exponential factor in
(
2.12
)
and (
2.13
)
is typically

\exp(C{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{d})\varepsilon^{-2})
, for
C
independent of
d
.

2.1.4.
Proof of Theorem

2.4

The proof relies on the following lemma, whose proof is also deferred to the end of the subsection.

Lemma 2.10
.

There exists a constant
C_{2}>0
, only depending on
d
,
T
,
\|Q^{\dagger}f\|_{1,\infty}

and
\|R^{\dagger}g\|_{1,\infty}
, such that,
{\mathbb{P}}
almost surely,

|h_{t}|\leq C_{2}\,;\quad|h_{t}^{n}|\leq C_{2},\quad t\in[0,T],\ n\geq 1.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Throughout,
C
is a generic constant that is allowed to vary from line to line,
as long as it only depends on
d
,
T
,
\|Q^{\dagger}f\|_{1,\infty}

and
\|R^{\dagger}g\|_{1,\infty}
.

First Step.

Invoking Lemma
2.10

and recalling that
\varpi\in(1,\sqrt{2}]
, we then have

(2.19)

\Bigl|{\tfrac{(1-\varpi^{-1})\varpi^{-n}}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}h_{t}^{n+1}}\Bigr|\leq C\varpi^{-n},\quad t\in[0,T],

from which we deduce (consider the difference between
(
2.9
)
and
the forward equation in (
2.11
))
that

(2.20)

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl|\overline{m}^{n}_{t}-m_{t}\bigr|\leq C\varpi^{-n}.

We now make the difference between
the backward equations in
(
2.10
) and
(
2.11
). We obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)=-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{t}^{n})-Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\eta_{t}\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varpi k_{t}^{n+1}\bigl(h_{t}^{n}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t

(2.21)

\displaystyle\hskip 75.0pt+\varpi\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)h_{t}^{\varpi}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t},

\displaystyle h_{T}^{n+1}-h_{T}^{\varpi}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\Bigl[R^{\dagger}g\bigl(\overline{m}_{T}^{n}\bigr)-R^{\dagger}g\bigl(m_{T}\bigr)\Bigr].

In particular, rewriting the above equation under
{\bm{W}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}={\bm{W}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{\varpi}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\Bigl(Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{t}^{n})-Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\eta_{t}\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varpi k_{t}^{n+1}\bigl(h_{t}^{n}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t

(2.22)

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\varepsilon\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{{\bm{h}}}/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T].

Then, taking the square, using
(
2.20
) and Lemmas

2.8
and

2.10
, expanding by Itô’s formula and applying Young’s inequality, we get

(2.23)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}&\geq-C\varpi^{-2n}\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|k_{t}\bigr|\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-C\bigl|k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\varepsilon^{2}\bigl|k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+2\varepsilon\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{{\bm{h}}}/\varepsilon}\bigr]\\
&\geq-C\varpi^{-2n}\mathrm{d}t-\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+2\varepsilon\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{{\bm{h}}}/\varepsilon}\bigr],\end{split}

for
t\in[0,T]

and for a constant
C
as in the statement.
For a free parameter
\lambda>1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Bigl[\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda t\bigr)\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\geq\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda t\bigr)\Bigl(\tfrac{C(\lambda-1)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}-C\varpi^{-2n}-\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t

(2.24)

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+2\varepsilon\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda t\bigr)\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-k_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\bigr].

And then, integrating from
t
to
T
, taking conditional expectation under
{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
given
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}

and recalling that

h_{T}^{n+1}=R^{\dagger}g(\overline{m}^{n}_{T})/\varpi

and

h_{T}^{\varpi}=R^{\dagger}g(m_{T})/\varpi
,
we get, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda t\bigr)\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{C(\lambda-1)}{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda s\bigr)\bigl|h_{s}^{n+1}-h_{s}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\biggr]

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\leq C\varpi^{-2n}\Bigl(\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda T\bigr)+\int_{t}^{T}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda s\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\Bigr)+\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda s\bigr)\bigl|h_{s}^{n}-h_{s}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\biggr].

Next, we choose
\lambda=4
. This yields

(2.26)

\begin{split}&\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{3C}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}t\bigr)\bigl|h_{t}^{n+1}-h_{t}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}s\bigr)\bigl|h_{s}^{n+1}-h_{s}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}(1+T)}{3}\varpi^{-2n}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}T\bigr)+\tfrac{1}{3}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}s\bigr)\bigl|h_{s}^{n}-h_{s}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\biggr].\end{split}

The above holds true for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
(under
{\mathbb{P}}
and

{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
)
and for every
t\in[0,T]
. By a standard induction argument,
using in addition Lemma

2.10
, we
deduce that, for a deterministic constant
C^{\prime}
depending on the same parameters as
C
,
for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, for every
t\in[0,T]

and for every
n\geq 1
,

(2.27)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}s\bigr)\bigl|h_{s}^{n}-h_{s}^{\varpi}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\biggr]&\leq C^{\prime}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}T\bigr)\sum_{k=0}^{n}3^{-k}\varpi^{-2(n-k)}\\
&=C^{\prime}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}T\bigr)\varpi^{-2n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl(\tfrac{\varpi^{2}}{3}\bigr)^{k}\\
&\leq C^{\prime}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}T\bigr)\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl(\tfrac{2}{3}\bigr)^{k}\leq C^{\prime}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{4C}{\varepsilon^{2}}T\bigr)\varpi^{-2n},\end{split}

where we used, in the last line, the fact that
\varpi\in(1,\sqrt{2}]
.
Because
\varepsilon
is less than 1, we can easily remove the constant
C^{\prime}
by
increasing the constant
C
.
And then,
by
(
2.25
), we obtain
(
2.12
).

Second Step.

We now turn to the proof of
(
2.13
). In fact, it
is a direct consequence of Pinsker’s inequality, which says that

d_{\textrm{\rm TV}}\bigl({\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},{\mathbb{P}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigr)\leq\sqrt{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\Bigl[\ln\Bigl(\frac{{\mathcal{E}}({\bm{h}}/\varepsilon)}{{\mathcal{E}}(\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon)}\Bigr)\Bigr]^{1/2},

where
d_{{\textrm{\rm TV}}}
in the left-hand side is the total variation distance.
Now,

\begin{split}&\ln\Bigl(\frac{{\mathcal{E}}({\bm{h}}/\varepsilon)}{{\mathcal{E}}(\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon)}\Bigr)=\ln\Bigl(\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(\varpi{\bm{h}}^{\varpi}/\varepsilon)}{{\mathcal{E}}(\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon)}\Bigr)\\
&=-\tfrac{\varpi}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(h_{s}^{\varpi}-h^{n}_{s}\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\tfrac{\varpi^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(|h_{s}^{\varpi}|^{2}-|h^{n}_{s}|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\\
&=-\tfrac{\varpi}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(h_{s}^{\varpi}-h^{n}_{s}\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}+\tfrac{\varpi^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(h_{s}^{\varpi}-h^{n}_{s}\bigr)\cdot h_{s}^{\varpi}\mathrm{d}s-\tfrac{\varpi^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(|h_{s}^{\varpi}|^{2}-|h^{n}_{s}|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\\
&=-\tfrac{\varpi}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(h_{s}^{\varpi}-h^{n}_{s}\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}+\tfrac{\varpi^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl|h_{s}^{\varpi}-h^{n}_{s}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

And then, by (
2.12
),

(2.28)

d_{\textrm{\rm TV}}\bigl({\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},{\mathbb{P}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigr)\leq\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon}\varpi^{-n}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr).

Modifying the constant
C
, we can easily get rid of the multiplicative constant
1/\varepsilon
in the right-hand side.
By (
2.12
) again, for any function

F
that is 1-bounded and 1-Lipschitz on the space
{\mathcal{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
,

\begin{split}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\Bigl[F\bigl(\overline{\bm{m}}^{n},{\bm{h}}^{n}\bigr)\Bigr]-{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}\Bigl[F\bigl({\bm{m}},{\bm{h}}\bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr|\leq\varpi^{-n}\exp\bigl(\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr),\end{split}

and then, by (
2.28
),
we get
(
2.13
).
∎

Remark 2.11
.

The reader can deduce from
the display
(
2.27
)
the reason why we assumed
\varpi
to be less than
\sqrt{2}
. In fact, even though

\varpi
were larger, the geometric decay would remain less
than
3^{-n}
because of the factor
1/3
in
(
2.26
).
Here, the factor
1/3
must be understood as
1/(\lambda-1)
for
\lambda=4
.
For sure, it would be tempting to choose
1/(\lambda-1)=1/\varpi
, but this would lead to

\lambda=\varpi+1
.
The resulting exponential factor

\exp(C\varepsilon^{-2}\lambda s)
in (
2.25
)
would become much too high with
\varpi
.

2.1.5.
Proof of the auxiliary statements

It now remains to prove Lemmas

2.1
,

2.10
and

2.8
and Remark

2.9
.

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

The proof mainly follows from the stochastic Pontryagin principle, see for instance
[
70
, chapter 3]
. Here, the stochastic Pontryagin principle provides
a necessary and sufficient condition on the dynamics of the optimal control because the cost coefficients are convex in the spatial variable.
However, the very fist step of the proof is to get rid of the parameter
\varpi
in the cost functional

{\mathcal{R}}^{\varpi X_{0}}(\varpi\bm{\alpha};\overline{\bm{m}}^{n};\varpi\varepsilon{\bm{W}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}/\varepsilon})
(see (
2.1
)).

For a control
\bm{\alpha}
, the controlled dynamics driven by
\varpi X_{0}
,
\varpi\bm{\alpha}
and the common noise

\varpi\varepsilon{\bm{W}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}

write

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{\varpi,\bm{\alpha}}=\varpi\alpha_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varpi\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T]\ ;\quad X_{0}^{\varpi,\bm{\alpha}}=\varpi X_{0}.

Dividing by
\varpi
, we can write
X_{t}^{\varpi,\bm{\alpha}}
in the form
X_{t}^{\varpi,\bm{\alpha}}=\varpi X_{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}
, with
(the notation
X_{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}
is in fact a bit abusive because the dynamics below still depend on
\varpi
)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}=\alpha_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T]\ ;\quad X_{0}^{\bm{\alpha}}=X_{0}.

Accordingly, the cost in
(
2.1
)
can be rewritten in the form

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigl[{\mathcal{R}}^{{\varpi X_{0}}}\bigl(\varpi{\bm{\alpha}};\overline{\bm{m}}^{n};\varpi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}{\bm{W}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigr)\bigr]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[\Bigl|\varpi X_{T}^{\bm{\alpha}}+g\bigl(\overline{m}_{T}^{n})\Bigr|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(\varpi^{2}|\alpha_{t}|^{2}+\bigl|\varpi X_{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}+f\bigl(\overline{m}_{t}^{n}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\biggr]\\
&=\frac{\varpi^{2}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[\Bigl|X_{T}^{\bm{\alpha}}+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}g\bigl(\overline{m}_{T}^{n})\Bigr|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(|\alpha_{t}|^{2}+\bigl|X_{t}^{\bm{\alpha}}+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{t}^{n}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\biggr]\end{split}

The leading parameter
\varpi^{2}
can be easily removed (because it does not change the minimizer).
Next, the stochastic Pontryagin principle says that
the optimal trajectory
to the cost functional in the above right-hand side
–and
thus the optimal trajectory to the cost functional
(
2.1
)
(but up to the rescaling factor
\varpi
)– is

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{n+1}=-\bigl(\eta_{t}X_{t}^{n+1}+h_{t}^{n+1}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}{\sigma}\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T]\ ;\quad X_{0}^{n+1}=X_{0},

with
(\eta_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and

{\bm{h}}^{n+1}
as in the statement.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.10
.

The proof is a straightforward consequence of
the two equations for

{\bm{h}}
and
{\bm{h}}^{n+1}
under
(respectively) the
probability measures

{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}

and

{\mathbb{P}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}
, see
(
2.15
) and
(
2.4
).
One can make the argument especially clear by using
the formulation presented in Remark

2.7

together with the fact that the coefficients
f
and
g
are bounded.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.8
.

In (
1.8
), we perform the change of variable

\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\varphi_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

We get

\begin{split}&\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)+\tfrac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\Delta^{2}_{xx}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)-\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl(\eta_{t}x+\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+Q^{\dagger}f(x)-\eta_{t}\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=0.\end{split}

Multiplying by
\varepsilon^{2}
and changing
(t,x)
into
(\varepsilon^{2}t,\varepsilon^{2}x)
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}_{xx}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)-\Bigl(\eta_{t}\varepsilon^{2}x+\theta_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\varepsilon^{2}t,\varepsilon^{2}x\bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\varepsilon^{2}Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\varepsilon^{2}x\bigr)-\varepsilon^{2}\eta_{t}\theta_{\varepsilon}\bigl(\varepsilon^{2}t,\varepsilon^{2}x\bigr)=0.\end{split}

Above,
(t,x)
belongs to
[0,T/\varepsilon^{2}]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. The terminal condition is

\varphi_{\varepsilon}(T/\varepsilon^{2},x)=g(\varepsilon^{2}x)
.
Moreover, by Lemma

2.10
, the function
\theta_{\varepsilon}
can be bounded independently
of
\varepsilon
. Then, for
t
at distance less than 1 from
T/\varepsilon^{2}
,
we get a bound for
\nabla_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}

from
standard estimates for
systems of nonlinear
parabolic PDEs, as used
in

[
29
]
. When
t
is at distance greater than 1 from
T/\varepsilon^{2}
,
we get a bound for
\nabla_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}

from interior estimates for
systems of nonlinear
parabolic PDEs, see for instance
[
30
]
.
The result follows.
∎

Proof of Remark
2.9
.

We now add a few lines in order to prove
the complementary
Remark

2.9
.
When
R
and
Q
are the identity matrices, the solution
{\bm{\eta}}
of the Riccati equation
(
2.3
)
becomes an
homothecy (which we identify with a real-valued function).
In turn,
if the functions
f
and
g
are ‘diagonal’, in the sense of
Remark
2.9
,
then the solution
\theta_{\varepsilon}
to the PDE
(
1.8
) is also diagonal,
namely
\theta^{i}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{i})
, with

\theta_{0}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
being the solution of the
1d
-equation:

\partial_{t}\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{0})+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\partial^{2}_{x_{0}x_{0}}\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{0})-\bigl(\eta_{t}x_{0}+\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{0})\bigr)\partial_{x_{0}}\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{0})+f_{0}(x_{0})-\eta_{t}\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{0})=0,

for
(t,x_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
and
with the terminal boundary condition

\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(T,x_{0})=g_{0}(x_{0})
.

In particular,

\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\bigl|\nabla_{x}\theta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\bigr|^{2}=d\sup_{(t,x_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}}\bigl|\partial_{x_{0}}\theta_{0,\varepsilon}(t,x_{0})\bigr|^{2}.

So, if we take for granted the fact that we cannot get better than
C_{0}/\varepsilon^{2}
for the right-hand side, then
the bound for the left-hand side becomes
C_{0}d/\varepsilon^{4}
. Returning back to
(
2.23
), the constant
C
therein
grows like
\sqrt{d}
with
d
, which completes the proof of the claim.
∎

2.2.
The common noise as an exploration noise

Following the agenda explained in the introduction, we now
regard the common noise as an exploration noise for an MFG without common noise.
In words,
\varepsilon
is set equal to
0
in the original MFG
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
)–(
1.3
)
and this is only in the choice of
the controls that we restore the presence of the common noise, in the form of a randomization.

2.2.1.
Presentation of the model

In the absence of common noise, the state dynamics merely write

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\beta_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t},\quad t\in[0,T].

However, we want
{\bm{\beta}}=(\beta_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

to be subjected to a random exploration of the form

\beta_{t}=\alpha_{t}+\varepsilon\dot{W}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],

where
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is the control effectively chosen by the agent (or by the ‘controller’) and
\varepsilon
is (strictly) positive (and is kept fixed throughout the subsection). In this expansion,

(\dot{W}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is formally understood as the time-derivative of
(W_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. Obviously, the latter does not exist as a function, which makes the above decomposition non tractable. However, it prompts us to introduce a variant based upon a mollification of the common noise. To make it clear, we introduce a family of regular processes
((W_{t}^{p})_{0\leq t\leq T})_{p\geq 1}
such that, almost surely (under
{\mathbb{P}}
),

\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|W_{t}-W_{t}^{p}|=0,

and, for any
p\geq 1
, the paths of
(W_{t}^{p})_{0\leq t\leq T}

are continuous and piecewise continuously differentiable. Throughout this subsection and the next one, we use
the following piecewise affine interpolation:

(2.29)

W_{t}^{p}:=W_{\tau_{p}(t)}+\frac{p(t-\tau_{p}(t))}{T}\bigl(W_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}-W_{\tau_{p}(t)}\bigr),

where, by definition,
\tau_{p}(t):=\lfloor pt/T\rfloor(T/p)
.
In words
\tau_{p}(t)
is the unique element of
(T/p)\cdot{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\tau_{p}(t)\leq t<\tau_{p}(t)+T/p=\tau_{p}(t+T/p)
, namely

\tau_{p}(t)=\ell T/p
, for
t\in[\ell T/p,(\ell+1)T/p)
and
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
.
It is worth observing that the time derivative of

{\bm{W}}^{p}
writes in the form
of finite differences of

{\bm{W}}
, which explains our definition of

{\bm{W}}^{p}
as a linear interpolation. We elaborate on this observation in Remark

2.12

below.

For an
{\mathbb{F}}^{{\bm{W}}}
-adapted and continuous environment

{\bm{m}}=(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, the cost functional, as originally defined in
(
1.2
), is turned into the following discrete time version

\widetilde{J}^{p}({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{m}}):=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\bigl|R^{\dagger}X_{T}+g(m_{T})\bigr|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl\{\bigl|Q^{\dagger}X_{\tau_{p}(t)}+f(m_{\tau_{p}(t)})\bigr|^{2}+|\alpha_{t}+\varepsilon\dot{W}_{t}^{p}|^{2}\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}t\biggr],

where the expectation is taken over both the idiosyncratic and exploration (common) noises.
Above, we require
{\bm{\alpha}}
to be progressively-measurable with respect to the filtration
{\mathbb{F}}^{p,X_{0},{\bm{B}},{\bm{W}}}:=(\sigma(X_{0},(B_{\tau_{p}(s)},W_{\tau_{p}(s)})_{s\leq t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}

and to be constant on any interval
[\ell T/p,(\ell+1)T/p)
, for
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
.
The above minimization problem can be regarded as a discrete-time control problem. The need for a time discretization is twofold:
(i)
On the one hand, it permits to avoid any anticipativity problem, since
the linear interpolation at a time
t\not=\tau_{p}(t)
anticipates on the future realization of the exploration noise;
(ii)
On the other hand, it is more adapted to numerical purposes.
To the best of our knowledge, the formulation of

\widetilde{J}^{p}({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{m}})
in the form of a cost functional featuring
an additive randomization of the control is new.

In the presence of the randomization, the cost functional might become very large as
p
tends to
\infty
, even for a control
{\bm{\alpha}}
of finite energy. This prompts us to renormalize
\widetilde{J}^{p}
.
As a result of the adaptability constraint, we indeed have

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\alpha_{t}\cdot\dot{W}_{t}^{p}\mathrm{d}t&={\mathbb{E}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\int_{\ell T/p}^{(\ell+1)T/p}\alpha_{t}\dot{W}_{t}^{p}\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\alpha_{\ell T/p}\cdot\bigl({W}_{(\ell+1)T/p}-{W}_{\ell T/p}\bigr)\bigr]=0,\end{split}

with the last line following from the fact that
\alpha_{\ell T/p}
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{\bm{W}}
-measurable.

Also, since
{\bm{W}}^{p}
is piecewise linear, we have

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}|\dot{W}_{t}^{p}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\int_{\ell T/p}^{(\ell+1)T/p}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl|\frac{p}{T}\bigl(W_{(\ell+1)T/p}-W_{\ell T/p}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t=d\,p\frac{T}{p}\frac{p^{2}}{T^{2}}\frac{T}{p}=d\,p.\end{split}

So, we must subtract to the cost a diverging term to recover the original cost functional. To make it clear,
the effective cost must be

(2.30)

\begin{split}J^{p}({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{m}}):=&\,\widetilde{J}^{p}({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{m}})-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}d\,p\\
=&\,\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\bigl|R^{\dagger}X_{T}+g(m_{T})\bigr|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl\{\bigl|Q^{\dagger}X_{\tau_{p}(t)}+f(m_{\tau_{p}(t)})\bigr|^{2}+|\alpha_{\tau_{p}(t)}|^{2}\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}t\biggr].\end{split}

Noticeably, we recover (up to the time discretization) the same cost functional
J
as in
(
1.2
), which explains why we have removed
the tilde over the symbol
J^{p}
.
Anyway,

J^{p}(\cdot;{\bm{m}})

and

\widetilde{J}^{p}(\cdot;{\bm{m}})

have the same minimizers.

Before we provide the form of the corresponding fictitious play, we need to clarify the
notion of tilted measure.
We assume that we are given a process
{\bm{h}}=(h_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
that is piecewise constant and
{\mathbb{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}
-adapted, with

{\mathbb{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}:={\mathbb{F}}^{{\bm{W}}^{p}}
: For the same
p

as before, the process
{\bm{h}}

is constant on each interval
([\ell T/p,(\ell+1)T/p))_{\ell=0,\cdots,p-1}
and each

h_{\ell T/p}
is

{\mathcal{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}_{\ell T/p}
-measurable,
with
{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}=\sigma(W^{p}_{s},s\leq\ell T/p)
. Then, as before, we can let

{\bm{W}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
:

W^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{t}=W_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}h_{s}\mathrm{d}s.

We compute the
p
-piecewise linear interpolation
{\bm{W}}^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
of
{\bm{W}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
:

\begin{split}W^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{t}&=W^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{\tau_{p}(t)}+\frac{p(t-\tau_{p}(t))}{T}\bigl(W^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}-W^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{\tau_{p}(t)}\bigr)\\
&=W^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{\tau_{p}(t)}+\frac{p(t-\tau_{p}(t))}{T}\bigl(W_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}-W_{\tau_{p}(t)}\bigr)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{p(t-\tau_{p}(t))}{T}\frac{T}{p}h_{\tau_{p}(t)}\\
&=W^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{\tau_{p}(t)}+\frac{p(t-\tau_{p}(t))}{T}\bigl(W_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}-W_{\tau_{p}(t)}\bigr)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigl(t-\tau_{p}(t)\bigr)h_{\tau_{p}(t)}\\
&=W^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{\tau_{p}(t)}+\int_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{t}\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{p}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{t}{h}_{s}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

from which we deduce that

(2.31)

W^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{t}=W^{p}_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}h_{s}\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\in[0,T].

For sure, under the probability
{\mathcal{E}}({\bm{h}}/\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathbb{P}}
,
the process

{\bm{W}}^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
is the piecewise linear interpolation of

{\bm{W}}^{{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}
and the latter is a Brownian motion. Also,

\dot{W}^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon}_{t}=\dot{W}^{p}_{t}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}h_{t},

which permits to say that
the trajectories controlled by

(\alpha_{t}+\varepsilon\dot{W}^{p}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
satisfy

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\bigl(\alpha_{t}-h_{t}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p,{\bm{h}}/\varepsilon},

which coincides with (
1.1
). Importantly, since

{\bm{h}}
is piecewise constant,

{\mathcal{E}}({\bm{h}}/\varepsilon)
can be expressed in terms of the sole
{\bm{h}}
and

{\bm{W}}^{p}
.

Remark 2.12
.

We feel useful to comment more on our choice to work with the linear interpolation
(W^{p}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

of
(W_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. While it may look somewhat arbitrary, this choice is in fact dictated by the structure of the
model and the form of the final result that is provided next. Indeed, the main result of this subsection, see Theorem
2.17
below, yields a bound for the weak error (in a convenient sense)
between the solution of a time-discrete version of the mean field game (with a common noise)
and the corresponding time-discrete variant of the fictitious play,
with the time mesh being given by
(kT/p)_{k=0,\cdots,p}
.

Obviously, working with a discrete-time version of the game makes perfect sense from a practical point of view. Here, it is also especially adapted to our objective.
As we already explained,
we want to regard the instantaneous value of the control at time

t
as being corrupted, at least formally, by the time derivative of the common noise.
Because the latter derivative does not exist as a true function, our strategy is to work instead with finite differences of the
common noise. This is one first reason for working with
(W^{p}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, because the linear interpolation
is completely characterized by the finite differences of
(W_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
at times

0
,
T/p
,
2T/p
,
\cdots
,
T
.
Another related reason is that, for
{\bm{h}}
a piecewise constant process that is

{\mathbb{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}
-adapted,
the relationship
(
2.31
)
holds true because

{\bm{W}}^{p}
and
{\bm{W}}^{p,{\bm{h}}}
are piecewise linear.
This is another strong case for working with the piecewise linear interpolation because
(
2.31
)
makes it possible to apply Girsanov theorem directly, with the latter being the key ingredient of the whole analysis.

In a nutshell, given the class of time-discrete games we address for approximating the original game
(using in particular finite differences to perturb the controls),
the linear interpolation is the most natural one to reconstruct time-continuous dynamics from time-discrete observations.
As such, the linear interpolation does not directly impact the bound on the weak error that is stated below, because the weak error
is defined a priori within the given class of approximating games, regardless of the choice of the interpolation.
However, working with the linear interpolation makes the proof much easier.

2.2.2.
Fictitious play

The analysis from the previous paragraph leads to the following new scheme, which should be regarded as the discrete-time analogue of
the scheme presented in
Subsection
2.1.1
.
Fix
a real
\varpi\in(1,\sqrt{2}]
and
an integer
p\geq 1

and, for the same
initialization
{\bm{m}}^{0}=(m^{0}_{t}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}))_{0\leq t\leq T}

and

{\bm{h}}^{0}=(h^{0}_{t}=0)_{0\leq t\leq T}
as in the continuous-time setting,
assume that we have defined two families of proxies
({\bm{m}}^{1},\cdots,{\bm{m}}^{n})

and
({\bm{h}}^{1},\cdots,{\bm{h}}^{n})
with the additional assumption
that each process
{\bm{h}}^{k}
is constant on each interval
[\ell T/p,(\ell+1)T/p)
, for

\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
and
k\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. And we assume each
(m_{\ell T/p}^{k},h_{\ell T/p}^{k})
to be measurable with respect to

{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}

if
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p\}
. Then, we solve for

(2.32)

\begin{split}{\bm{\alpha}}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}&=\textrm{\rm argmin}_{\bm{\alpha}}\biggl({\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[{\mathcal{R}}^{p,\varpi X_{0}}\Bigl(\varpi{\bm{\alpha}}+\varpi\varepsilon\dot{\bm{W}}^{p,\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon};\overline{\bm{m}}^{n};0\Bigr)\Bigr]-\tfrac{1}{2}d\,\varpi^{2}\varepsilon^{2}p\biggr)\\
&=\textrm{\rm argmin}_{\bm{\alpha}}\biggl({\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[{\mathcal{R}}^{p,\varpi X_{0}}\Bigl(\varpi{\bm{\alpha}};\overline{\bm{m}}^{n};\varpi\varepsilon{\bm{W}}^{p,\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}/\varepsilon}\Bigr)\Bigr]\biggr),\end{split}

with the same measurability rules as those explained above and where

{\mathcal{R}}^{p,x_{0}}
is the obvious discrete-time version of

{\mathcal{R}}^{x_{0}}
, namely

(2.33)

{\mathcal{R}}^{p,x_{0}}({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{n}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}{\bm{w}})=\frac{1}{2}\biggl[\bigl|Rx_{T}+g(n_{T})\bigr|^{2}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\Bigl\{\bigl|Qx_{\ell T/p}+f(n_{\ell T/p})\bigr|^{2}+\bigl|\alpha_{\ell T/p}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr\}\biggr],

where

x_{(\ell+1)T/p}=x_{\ell T/p}+(T/p)\alpha_{\ell T/p}+\sigma(B_{(\ell+1)T/p}-B_{\ell T/p})+\varepsilon(w_{(\ell+1)T/p}-w_{\ell T/p})
.
As before,

\overline{m}^{n}_{t}
in
(
2.32
)
is

\overline{m}^{n}_{t}=\tfrac{\varpi(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varpi^{-k}m_{t}^{k}
.

The first step here is to solve the optimization problem
(
2.32
).
Very similar to Lemma
2.1
, we
can provide an explicit form for the optimal feedback
through the solution of the following time-discrete
Riccati equation:

(2.34)

\begin{split}&\frac{p}{T}\Bigl(\eta_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}-\eta_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\Bigr)-\eta_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}\eta_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}+Q^{\dagger}Q\Bigl(I_{d}-\frac{T}{p}\eta_{\ell T/p}\Bigr)=0,\\
&\hskip 250.0pt\quad\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-2\}\ ;\\
&\frac{p}{T}\Bigl(\eta_{T}^{(p)}-\eta_{(p-1)T/p}^{(p)}\Bigr)-\eta_{T}^{(p)}\eta_{(p-1)T/p}^{(p)}=0\ ;\quad\eta_{T}^{(p)}=R^{\dagger}R.\end{split}

The analysis of the latter (see the earlier reference

[
35
]
) goes through
the auxiliary Riccati equation

(2.35)

\frac{p}{T}\Bigl(P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}-P_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\Bigr)-P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}\Bigl(I_{d}+\frac{T}{p}P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}\Bigr)^{-1}P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}+Q^{\dagger}Q=0,

for
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
,
with
P_{T}^{(p)}=R^{\dagger}R
as boundary condition.
The Riccati equation (
2.35
) can be solved inductively: the solution is symmetric
and non-negative
9
9

9

Symmetry is obvious. Non-negativity is a bit more demanding. Assuming that
P^{(p)}_{(\ell+1)T/p}
is non-negative, non-negativity of

P_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}
is proved as follows.
For any vector
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\begin{split}\bigl(P_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}x\bigr)\cdot x&=\bigl(P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}x\bigr)\cdot x-\tfrac{T}{p}\bigl(P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}x\bigr)\cdot\Bigl[\bigl(I_{d}+\tfrac{T}{p}P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}\bigr)^{-1}P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}x\Bigr]+\tfrac{T}{p}|Qx|^{2}\\
&\geq\bigl(P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}x\bigr)\cdot x-\bigl(P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}x\bigr)\cdot x+\bigl(P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}x\bigr)\cdot\Bigl[\bigl(I_{d}+\tfrac{T}{p}P_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}\bigr)^{-1}x\Bigr],\end{split}

and the right-hand side is obviously non-negative.
, which guarantees
that the inverse right above is well-defined.
Then,

(2.36)

\eta_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}=\Bigl(I_{d}+\frac{T}{p}P^{(p)}_{(\ell+1)T/p}\Bigr)^{-1}P^{(p)}_{(\ell+1)T/p},\quad\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\},

solves
(
2.34
). Notice that the above left-hand side is symmetric and non-negative because the two matrices in the right-hand side commute.

Lemma 2.13
.

Under the above assumptions, the
minimization problem
(
2.32
)
has a unique solution

{\bm{\alpha}}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}
, which writes

(2.37)

\alpha_{t}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}=-\biggl(\eta_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}X_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}+\widetilde{h}_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{n+1}\biggr),\quad t\in[0,T],

where

\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{n+1}
solves the backward SDE:

(2.38)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}=\Bigl\{-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}^{n}\bigr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}_{\tau_{p}(t)}\Bigr]{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt+\Bigl(\eta_{\tau_{p(t)}+T/p}^{(p)}+\frac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\Bigr)\widetilde{h}_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{n+1}\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 50.0pt+\varpi k_{t}^{n+1}h_{t}^{n}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}^{n+1}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T),\\
&\widetilde{h}_{T}^{n+1}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}R^{\dagger}g\bigl(\overline{m}^{n}_{T}\bigr).\end{split}

Accordingly, the optimal path
{\bm{X}}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}
solves
(up to a rescaling factor
\varpi
)
the forward SDE:

(2.39)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}=\alpha_{t}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p,\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T];\quad X_{0}^{n+1}=X_{0}.

Remark 2.14
.

Although
it is not indicated in
the notations

\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{n}
,

{\bm{h}}^{n}
,

{\bm{k}}^{n}

and
\overline{\bm{m}}^{n}
,
it should be clear to the reader that
the latter four processes depend on
p
,
\varpi
and
\varepsilon
.

Remark 2.15
.

The convergence of

(P^{(p)}_{\tau_{p}(t)})_{0\leq t\leq T}
to

(\eta_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
in Lemma
2.1
is obvious.

By the symmetric and non-negative structure of
the matrices
(P^{(p)}_{\ell})_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}
, we can easily
get uniform bounds on
the latter. In turn,
we can
regard the equation (
2.35
) as an Euler scheme for
a matricial differential equation driven by a Lipschitz vector field. The rate of convergence is linear in

p
. By
(
2.36
), we deduce that

(2.40)

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\eta_{t}^{(p)}-\eta_{t}|\leq\frac{C}{p},

for a constant
C
independent of
p
, but possibly depending on the dimension
d
. Typically, the bound on
(P^{(p)}_{\ell})_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}
should depend on the norms of

Q^{\dagger}Q
and
R^{\dagger}R
and is thus expected to depend on
d
in a polynomial way. In turn, stability arguments for finite difference equations say
that
those bounds should propagate to the constant
C
in the above estimate for the distance between
(\eta^{(p)}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and

(\eta_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. However, this argument may not be sharp: when
Q
and
R
are the identity matrix, the Riccati equation
(
2.35
)
reduces to a scalar equation and the constant
C
should just scale like
\sqrt{d}
.

Remark 2.16
.

The backward SDE (
2.38
) is in fact a mere discrete-time equation. Indeed,

(2.41)

\begin{split}\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}&=\widetilde{h}_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{n+1}+\tfrac{T}{\varpi p}Q^{\dagger}f(\overline{m}_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{n}){\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\tfrac{T}{p}\Bigl(\eta_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\Bigr)\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}-\varepsilon\int_{\ell T/p}^{(\ell+1)T/p}k_{s}^{n+1}\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\end{split}

for

\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
. Taking
conditional expectation given
{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{{\bm{W}}}
,
we can solve for

\Bigl(I_{d}+\tfrac{T}{p}\eta_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T^{2}}{p^{2}}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\Bigr)\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}.

It is easy to deduce
\widetilde{h}^{n+1}_{\ell T/p}
.
We get

(2.42)

\begin{split}\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}&=\Bigl(I_{d}+\tfrac{T}{p}\eta_{(\ell+1)/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T^{2}}{p^{2}}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\Bigr)^{-1}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\times{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[\widetilde{h}_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{n+1}+\tfrac{T}{\varpi p}Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{n}\bigr){\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{{\bm{W}}}\biggr],\end{split}

which proves, by induction, that

\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}

is

{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}
-measurable.
It suffices to observe that, for
Z
an
{\mathcal{F}}_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}
-measurable random variable, the conditional expectation of
Z
given
{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{\bm{W}}

is in fact

{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}
-measurable.

Taking for granted Lemma
2.13
(the proof is given at the end of the subsection), we put

(2.43)

h^{n+1}_{t}:=\widetilde{h}^{n+1}_{\tau_{p}(t)},

which satisfies the required measurability constraints thanks to
Remark

2.16
.
Then, we let

(2.44)

\begin{split}m^{n+1}_{t}&:={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[X_{t}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}})\bigr]={\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}/\varepsilon}\bigl[X_{t}^{(p),n+1,\varpi}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}})\bigr],\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

together with

(2.45)

\overline{m}^{n+1}_{t}=\tfrac{\varpi(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\varpi^{-k}m_{t}^{k}=\tfrac{\varpi^{-n}(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}m^{n+1}_{t}+\Bigl(1-\tfrac{\varpi^{-n}(1-\varpi^{-1})}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}\Bigr)\overline{m}^{n}_{t}.

Importantly, notice that

m^{n+1}_{\ell T/p}={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[X^{(p),n+1,\varpi}_{\ell T/p}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}}^{p})\bigr]={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[X^{(p),n+1,\varpi}_{\ell T/p}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}\bigr],\quad\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p\},

which proves in particular that the left-hand side is

{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}}
-measurable.

The analogue of Theorem

2.4
becomes:

Theorem 2.17
.

There exists
a threshold
c>0
, depending on
d
,
T
, the norms

\|f\|_{1,\infty}
and
\|g\|_{1,\infty}

and the norms
|Q|
and
|R|
of the matrices
Q
and
R
, such that,
for
p\varepsilon^{2}\geq c
, the
scheme
(
2.43
)–(
2.45
)
converges
to

({\bm{m}}^{(p)},\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varpi,{\bm{k}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varpi)
,
where
({\bm{m}}^{(p)},\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)},{\bm{k}}^{(p)})
is the unique solution of the decoupled discrete-time FBSDE system:

(2.46)

\begin{split}&m_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}=m_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}-\tfrac{T}{p}\eta_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}m_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}+\varepsilon\bigl(W_{(\ell+1)T/p}-W_{\ell T/p}\bigr),\\
&\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}=\widetilde{h}_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}\bigr){\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\\
&\hskip 30.0pt-\tfrac{T}{p}\Bigl(\eta_{(\ell+1)T/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{\ell\leq p-2\}}\Bigr)\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}-\biggl(\int_{\ell T/p}^{(\ell+1)T/p}k_{s}^{(p)}\mathrm{d}s\biggr)\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\\
&\hskip 30.0pt-\varepsilon\int_{\ell T/p}^{(\ell+1)T/p}k_{s}^{(p)}\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\},\\
&m_{0}^{(p)}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}),\quad\widetilde{h}_{T}^{(p)}=R^{\dagger}g\bigl(m_{T}^{(p)}\bigr),\end{split}

with an explicit bound on the rate of convergence, namely

(2.47)

\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega}\Bigl[\sup_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}\Bigl(|m_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}-\overline{m}_{\ell T/p}^{n}|^{2}+|\varpi^{-1}\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}-h_{\ell T/p}^{n}|^{2}\Bigr)\Bigr]\leq\varpi^{-2n}\exp\bigl(C\varepsilon^{-2}\bigr),

for a constant
C
that also depends on
d
,
T
,

\|f\|_{1,\infty}
,
\|g\|_{1,\infty}
,
|Q|
and
|R|
.

Moreover,
if we extend
{\bm{m}}^{(p)}
by continuous interpolation to the entire
[0,T]

and if we call
{\bm{h}}^{(p)}
the piecewise constant extension of
\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}

to the entire
[0,T]
, then,
up to a modification of the constant
C
, the weak error of the scheme for the
Fortet-Mourier distance satisfies

(2.48)

\begin{split}&\sup_{F}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[F\bigl(\overline{\bm{m}}^{n},{\bm{h}}^{n}\bigr)\Bigr]-{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[F\bigl({\bm{m}}^{(p)},\varpi^{-1}{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr|\leq\varpi^{-n}\exp\bigl(C\varepsilon^{-2}\bigr),\end{split}

the supremum being taken over all the functions

F
on
{\mathcal{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
that are
bounded by
1
and
1
-Lipschitz continuous.

The following comments are in order:

•

In
(
2.46
),

{\bm{m}}^{(p)}
and
\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}

are implicitly understood as

{\bm{m}}^{(p)}=(m_{\ell T/p}^{(p)})_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}

and

\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}=(h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)})_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}
,
with
m_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}

and
h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}
being
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued and

{\mathcal{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}_{\ell T/p}
-measurable for each
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p\}
.

•

The process
{\bm{k}}^{(p)}
is understood as
(k_{s}^{(p)})_{0\leq s\leq T}
. It is
{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
-valued and

{\mathbb{F}}^{\bm{W}}
-progressively measurable.

Existence and uniqueness of a solution to
(
2.46
) is in fact ensured by the following proposition, whose proof is deferred to
§
2.2.5
. The statement also explains the need for a threshold on the product
p\varepsilon^{2}

in the two bounds (
2.47
)
and (
2.48
).

Proposition 2.18
.

There exists a constant
c
, depending on
d
,
T
,

\|f\|_{1,\infty}
,
\|g\|_{1,\infty}
,
|Q|
and
|R|
,
such that for
p\varepsilon^{2}\geq c
,
the backward equation in
(
2.46
)
admits a unique solution

(\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)})_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}
,
with
\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\in L^{2}(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{p,{\bm{W}}},{\mathbb{P}};{\mathbb{R}}^{d})

for each
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p\}
.

The process
(k_{t}^{(p)})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is
{\mathbb{F}}^{\bm{W}}
-progressively measurable and is square integrable over
[0,T]\times\Omega
(
\Omega
being equipped with
{\mathbb{P}}
).

We also feel useful to clarify the meaning of the continuous interpolation of
{\bm{m}}^{(p)}
in (
2.48
). In fact, the definition
is similar to (
2.29
):

(2.49)

m_{t}^{(p)}:=m_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}+\frac{p(t-\tau_{p}(t))}{T}\bigl(m^{(p)}_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}-m^{(p)}_{\tau_{p}(t)}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T].

Moreover, the definition of the piecewise constant extension of
\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}

is similar to
(
2.43
):

(2.50)

h^{(p)}_{t}:=\widetilde{h}^{(p)}_{\tau_{p}(t)},\quad t\in[0,T].

It is worth noting that
({\bm{m}}^{(p)},{\bm{h}}^{(p)})
(hence extended to the entire
[0,T]
as
above) is the unique solution of the
following two-step fixed point problem, which is nothing but
the
discrete-time version of the MFG
with common noise addressed in Theorem

2.4
:

(i)
The solution of

(2.51)

\textrm{\rm argmin}_{\bm{\alpha}}\biggl({\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[{\mathcal{R}}^{p,X_{0}}\Bigl({\bm{\alpha}}+\varepsilon\dot{\bm{W}}^{p,{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon};{\bm{m}}^{(p)};0\Bigr)\Bigr]-\tfrac{1}{2}d\,\varepsilon^{2}p\biggr),

which is also

(2.52)

\textrm{\rm argmin}_{\bm{\alpha}}\biggl({\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigl[{\mathcal{R}}^{p,X_{0}}\Bigl({\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{m}}^{(p)};\varepsilon{\bm{W}}^{p,{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigr)\Bigr]\biggr)

is given by

(2.53)

\alpha_{t}^{(p),\star}=-\biggl(\eta_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}X_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p),\star}+h_{t}^{(p)}\biggr),\quad t\in[0,T],

where

(2.54)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{(p),\star}&=-\eta_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}X_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p),\star}\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p}\\
&=\alpha_{t}^{(p),\star}\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p,{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with
X_{0}
as initial condition.

(ii)
The process obtained by taking the conditional expectation of
{\bm{X}}^{(p),\star}
given
\sigma({\bm{W}}^{p})
, namely

({\mathbb{E}}[X_{t}^{(p),\star}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}}^{p})])_{0\leq t\leq T}
, solves the forward equation in
(
2.46
), i.e.,

(2.55)

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[X_{\ell T/p}^{(p),\star}\,|\,\sigma({\bm{W}}^{p})\bigr]=m^{(p)}_{\ell T/p},\quad\ell=0,\cdots,p.

Indeed, by a straightforward adaptation of Lemma

2.13
,
the system
(
2.46
)
can be shown
to characterize the solution to the fixed point problem associated with the two items

(i)
and
(ii)
right above. This proves in particular that
the discrete-time MFG
constructed on the top of the cost functional
(
2.51
) has a unique solution when
p\varepsilon^{2}\geq c
, which is
given by Proposition

2.18
.

Remark 2.19
.

As made in clear in the forthcoming proof of Proposition

2.18
, there is another conceivable extension for

\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}
(in addition to the extension
defined in (
2.50
)).
Indeed, very similar to
(
2.38
), given
{\bm{h}}^{(p)}

(from
(
2.50
)), one can
define the extension of

\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}

to the entire
[0,T]
as the solution
of the
BSDE:

(2.56)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}=\Bigl\{-{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}^{(p)}\bigr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}_{\tau_{p}(t)}\Bigr]{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt+\Bigl(\eta_{\tau_{p(t)}+T/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\Bigr)h_{t}^{(p)}\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 50.0pt+k_{t}^{(p)}h_{t}^{(p)}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}^{(p)}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T),\\
&\widetilde{h}_{T}^{(p)}=R^{\dagger}g\bigl(m^{(p)}_{T}\bigr).\end{split}

Consistency of this extension is explained in the proof of
Proposition

2.18
. The time-continuous
process
\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{p}
defined as the solution of
(
2.56
)
coincides at times
(\ell T/p)_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}

with the solution

(\widetilde{h}_{\ell T/p}^{(p)})_{\ell=0,\cdots,p}
of
(
2.46
). Moreover,
the process
{\bm{k}}^{(p)}
in
(
2.56
) coincides with

{\bm{k}}^{(p)}
in
(
2.46
).

Remark 2.20
.

Similar to the proof of Theorem

2.4
,
the proof of
Theorem
2.17

also shows that

d_{\textrm{\rm TV}}\bigl({\mathbb{P}}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},{\mathbb{P}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigr)\leq\varpi^{-n}\exp\bigl(C\varepsilon^{-2}\bigr).

Remark 2.21
.

Following
Remark

2.9

about the scope of Theorem

2.4
to the higher dimensional setting, we could think of tracking the dependence of the constant

C
(in (
2.11
)
and (
2.12
)), which is here independent of
p
, upon the dimension
d
.
Although we prefer not to address this question in full detail, we
insist on the fact that the conclusion of Remark
2.9

also holds true here, meaning that the constant
C
cannot be better than

O(\exp(O(\sqrt{d})))
in simple cases when
Q
and
R
are the identity matrices and
f
and
g
are diagonal.
Intuitively, we cannot expect a constant that would be, asymptotically in
d
and uniformly in
p
, better than the constant appearing
in the statement of
Theorem

2.4
, as otherwise we could take the limit
p\rightarrow\infty
in the statement of Theorem

2.17
and then get a better estimate in Theorem

2.4
.

2.2.3.
Analysis of the convergence

Conditioning on

{\bm{W}}
in
(
2.39
) and recalling
the two notations
(
2.43
)
and
(
2.44
), we obtain

\mathrm{d}m_{t}^{n+1}=-\Bigl(\eta^{(p)}_{\tau_{p}(t)}m_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{n+1}+h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p,\varpi{\bm{h}}^{n}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T];\quad m_{0}^{n+1}={\mathbb{E}}(X_{0}).

Thanks to
(
2.45
), the identity (
2.9
) becomes

(2.57)

\mathrm{d}\overline{m}_{t}^{n+1}=-\bigl(\eta_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}\overline{m}_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{n+1}+\tfrac{(1-\varpi^{-1})\varpi^{-n}}{1-\varpi^{-(n+1)}}h_{t}^{n+1}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p},\quad t\in[0,T].

As in the
analysis of (
2.10
), the second term in the middle disappears asymptotically at a geometric rate.
This follows from the next result, which is an improved version of Lemma
2.10
and which is also used next
in place of Lemma

2.8
(the latter relying on a Markovian structure that is not satisfied here):

Lemma 2.22
.

With
(\overline{\bm{m}}^{n},\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{n})

being defined as in
(
2.38
),
(
2.44
)
and (
2.45
)
and

({\bm{m}}^{(p)},\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)})

as in
(
2.46
)
and
(
2.56
),
there exists a constant
C_{1}
, only depending on
d
,
T
,

\|f\|_{1,\infty}
,
\|g\|_{1,\infty}
,
|Q|
and
|R|
, such that,
{\mathbb{P}}
almost surely,

(2.58)

|h_{t}^{n}|\leq C_{1},\quad t\in[0,T],\ n\geq 1\,;\quad|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}|\leq C_{1},\quad t\in[0,T].

Moreover, for the same constant
C_{1}
, there exists
\delta\in(0,1]
, depending on the same parameters as
C_{1}
, such that, with probability 1 under

{\mathbb{P}}
,

(2.59)

\forall t\in[0,T],\quad{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\delta\varepsilon^{2}\int_{t}^{T}|k_{s}^{(p)}|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{{\bm{W}}}\biggr]\leq C_{1}.

The proof of Lemma
2.22
is deferred to §
2.2.5
.
Taking for granted the statement, we now complete the proof of Theorem

2.17
.

Proof of Theorem
2.17
.

Taking for granted Proposition

2.18
, we assume that the system
(
2.46
)
has a unique solution. Implicitly, this requires
p\varepsilon^{2}\geq c
, but this is indeed one of the assumption
of Theorem
2.17
.

First Step.

For any integer
n\geq 1
, we thus compare
(\overline{\bm{m}}^{n+1},\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{n+1})
and

(\overline{\bm{m}}^{(p)},\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varpi)
,
with the latter two ones being extended to the entire
[0,T]
as explained in
(
2.49
) and
(
2.56
). To do so, we first notice that

\mathrm{d}m_{t}^{(p)}=-\eta_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}m_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{p},\quad t\in[0,T].

By
(
2.57
)
and
(
2.58
),

\mathrm{d}\bigl(\overline{m}^{n+1}_{t}-{m}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)=-\eta_{\tau_{p}(t)}^{(p)}\bigl(\overline{m}_{t}^{n+1}-{m}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t-O\bigl(\varpi^{-n}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t,\quad t\in[0,T],

where
|O(\varpi^{-n})|\leq C\varpi^{-n}
for a constant
C
as in the statement of Theorem
2.17
.
We easily get

(2.60)

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\overline{m}^{n+1}_{t}-{m}_{t}^{(p)}|\leq C\varpi^{-n}.

Second Step.
We turn to the difference
\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{n+1}-\widetilde{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varpi
.
We rewrite
(
2.38
) as

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}=\Bigl\{-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(\overline{m}_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}^{n}\bigr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}_{\tau_{p}(t)}\Bigr]{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt+\Bigl(\eta_{\tau_{p(t)}+T/p}^{(p)}+\tfrac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\Bigr)h_{t}^{n+1}\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 50.0pt+\varpi k_{t}^{n+1}\bigl(h_{t}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon k_{t}^{n+1}\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

Similarly, we rewrite
(
2.56
)
as

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)=\Bigl\{-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[Q^{\dagger}f\bigl(m_{\tau_{p}(t)+T/p}^{(p)}\bigr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{p,{\bm{W}}}_{\tau_{p}(t)}\Bigr]{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt+\Bigl(\eta_{\tau_{p(t)}+T/p}^{(p)}+\frac{T}{p}Q^{\dagger}Q{\mathbf{1}}_{\{t\leq(p-1)T/p\}}\Bigr)\bigl(\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 50.0pt+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{\varpi{\bm{h}}^{(p),\varpi}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon},\quad t\in[0,T),\\
&\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{T}^{(p)}=\tfrac{1}{\varpi}R^{\dagger}g\bigl(m^{(p)}_{T}\bigr).\end{split}

Forming and squaring the difference between the two equations and
using
(
2.60
) (together with Remark

2.15
, which gives a bound for
{\bm{\eta}}^{(p)}
independent of
p
), we deduce
(very like as in
(
2.22
)
and
(
2.23
))

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\Bigl[\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\\
&\geq-C\varpi^{-2n}\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|h^{n+1}_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|\widetilde{h}^{n+1}_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-C|k_{t}^{(p)}|\,\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|\,\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|\bigl|k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\varepsilon^{2}\bigl|k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigr],\end{split}

with the constant
C
being allowed to vary from line to line as long as it depends on the same parameters as those indicated in the statement of
Theorem

2.17
.
With
\delta
as in
(
2.59
), this leads to

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\Bigl[\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\\
&\geq-C\varpi^{-2n}\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|h^{n+1}_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-C\bigl|\widetilde{h}^{n+1}_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\tfrac{C}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-\bigl(\delta\varepsilon^{2}|k_{t}^{(p)}|^{2}+\tfrac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\bigl|k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\varepsilon\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\bigr].\end{split}

Using the fact that, by convention,
\varepsilon
is taken
in
(0,1)
, we can get rid of the last term on the first line (which is dominated by the last term on the second line). Then, letting

(2.61)

E_{t}^{(p)}:=\exp\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\delta\varepsilon^{2}|k_{s}^{(p)}|^{2}+\tfrac{C}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\biggr),\quad t\in[0,T],

and assuming without any loss of generality that
\delta\in(0,1)
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Bigl[E_{t}^{(p)}\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\geq-CE_{t}^{(p)}\Bigl[\varpi^{-2n}+\bigl|h^{n+1}_{t}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{t}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}E_{t}^{(p)}\bigl|k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

(2.62)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.0pt+\varepsilon E_{t}^{(p)}\bigl(h_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\cdot\Bigl[\bigl(k_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{t}^{(p)}\bigr)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\Bigr].

Then, using in addition the fact that

\widetilde{h}_{T}^{n+1}-\widetilde{h}_{T}^{(p)}/\varpi=[g(\overline{m}_{T}^{n})-g(m_{T}^{(p)})]/\varpi
, we get

\begin{split}&E_{t}^{(p)}\bigl|\widetilde{h}_{t}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}\widetilde{h}_{t}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}E_{s}^{(p)}\bigl|k_{s}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}k_{s}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{\bm{W}}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 5.0pt\leq C{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}/\varepsilon}\biggl[E_{T}^{(p)}\varpi^{-2n}+\int_{t}^{T}E_{s}^{(p)}\Bigl[\varpi^{-2n}+\bigl|h^{n+1}_{s}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{s}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{s}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{s}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{\bm{W}}\biggr].\end{split}

Take now
t=\ell T/p
, for some
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,p-1\}
. Then,
with
C
and
\delta
as above,

\begin{split}&E^{(p)}_{\ell T/p}\bigl|h_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\\
&\leq C{\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[E_{T}^{(p)}\varpi^{-2n}+\int_{\ell T/p}^{T}E_{s}^{(p)}\Bigl[\bigl|h^{n+1}_{s}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{s}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{s}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{s}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}s\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{\bm{W}}\biggr]\\
&\leq C\exp\biggl(\delta\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{\ell T/p}|k_{s}^{(p)}|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\biggr){\mathbb{E}}^{{\bm{h}}^{(p)}\hskip-2.0pt/\varepsilon}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\delta\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\ell T/p}^{T}|k_{s}^{(p)}|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\ell T/p}^{\bm{W}}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 1.0pt\times\biggl(\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{T}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\varpi^{-2n}+\tfrac{T}{p}\sum_{k=\ell}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\Bigl[\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr]\biggr).\end{split}

By
(
2.59
), we
have a bound for the expectation on the
penultimate line. Also, recalling the
form of
E^{(p)}_{\ell T/p}
in
(
2.61
), we
can divide
both sides of the inequality
by

\exp(\delta\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{\ell T/p}|k_{s}^{(p)}|^{2}\mathrm{d}s)
. We
get

\begin{split}&\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\bigl|h_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\phantom{\Bigl(}\\
&\leq C\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{T}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\varpi^{-2n}+\tfrac{CT}{p}\sum_{k=\ell}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\Bigl[\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\Bigr].\end{split}

For
CT/p\leq 1/2
(which assumption is consistent with the lower bound
p\varepsilon^{2}\geq c
), we get
(for a possibly new value of
C
)

\begin{split}&\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\bigl|h_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\leq A_{\ell}+\tfrac{CT}{p}\sum_{k=\ell+1}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2},\end{split}

with the notation

A_{\ell}:=C\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{T}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)+\tfrac{CT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k=\ell}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}.

By the discrete version of Gronwall’s lemma, we obtain
(for a possibly new value of
C
):

(2.63)

\begin{split}&\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega}\bigl|h_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq CA_{\ell}\leq C\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{T}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)+\tfrac{CT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k=\ell}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}.\end{split}

And then, for some real
\lambda>0
,

\begin{split}&\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(\lambda\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\bigl|h_{\ell T/p}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{\ell T/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\\
&\leq C\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{T}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\exp\bigl(\lambda\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\varpi^{-2n}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\tfrac{CT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\biggl(\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\exp\bigl(\lambda\tfrac{\ell T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\biggr)\exp\bigl(C\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}\\
&\leq\tfrac{C}{\exp(\lambda T/(p\delta\varepsilon^{2}))-1}\exp\bigl((C+\lambda)\tfrac{T}{\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\varpi^{-2n}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\tfrac{CT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\tfrac{\exp(\lambda T/(p\delta\varepsilon^{2}))}{\exp(\lambda T/(p\delta\varepsilon^{2}))-1}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\exp\bigl((C+\lambda)\tfrac{kT}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr)\underset{\small\omega\in\Omega}{\textrm{\rm essup}}\bigl|h_{kT/p}^{n}-\tfrac{1}{\varpi}h_{kT/p}^{(p)}\bigr|^{2}.\end{split}

Now,

\begin{split}\tfrac{T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\tfrac{\exp(\lambda T/(p\delta\varepsilon^{2}))}{\exp(\lambda T/(p\delta\varepsilon^{2}))-1}&=\tfrac{T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\tfrac{1}{1-\exp(-\lambda T/(p\delta\varepsilon^{2}))}=\tfrac{1}{\lambda}\varphi\bigl(\tfrac{\lambda T}{p\delta\varepsilon^{2}}\bigr),\end{split}

where
